<a id="shelf-c3d4f6314b96"></a>
### War in Human Civilization — Azar Gat

Status: **recommended**. Sources: 263, 265.

Connects conflict to subsistence, demography, state formation and industrial change. Broad comparative evidence supports reusable constraints, while evolutionary explanations and causal generalization warrant caution. War is central; examples add historical knowledge rather than merely repeating a thesis.

Eligibility: nonfiction explanatory work, with no established hard exclusion or serious veto in the inspected source report and available bibliographic evidence. Overall and density confidence: **low**; no full-text density audit is claimed.

<a id="shelf-5a126b79b9e4"></a>
### Political Order and Political Decay — Francis Fukuyama

Status: **recommended**. Sources: 263, 265.

Compares state capacity, accountability, rule of law and institutional decay across regions. Contrasting development paths offer useful boundary conditions. Broad synthesis necessarily compresses disputes; politics remains the center despite strong strategic utility.

Eligibility: nonfiction explanatory work, with no established hard exclusion or serious veto in the inspected source report and available bibliographic evidence. Overall and density confidence: **low**; no full-text density audit is claimed.

<a id="shelf-942fb9f788ff"></a>
### The World Imagined — Hendrik Spruyt

Status: **recommended**. Sources: 263, 265.

Compares Islamic, Chinese and Southeast Asian orders to show how shared beliefs shape diplomacy and authority. Different regional cases challenge a universal Westphalian model. Thematic inference is more secure than any single causal estimate; endpoint 1900 approximates the main premodern and nineteenth-century comparison. https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/world-imagined/0C9C358AE86CF6B231B5D3484959B0FA/listing

Eligibility: nonfiction explanatory work, with no established hard exclusion or serious veto in the inspected source report and available bibliographic evidence. Overall and density confidence: **low**; no full-text density audit is claimed.

<a id="shelf-b83405b6ac94"></a>
### The Collapse of Complex Societies — Joseph A. Tainter

Status: **recommended**. Sources: 259, 263, 265.

Explains collapse through declining marginal returns to increasing organizational complexity. Comparisons expose resource costs and institutional constraints; the framework is useful but is not a universal law and causal identification is limited. Distinct cases justify substantial detail. Cross-period comparison has no single endpoint. https://books.google.com/books?id=M4H-02d9oE0C

Eligibility: nonfiction explanatory work, with no established hard exclusion or serious veto in the inspected source report and available bibliographic evidence. Overall and density confidence: **low**; no full-text density audit is claimed.
